low all warnings and wear any PPE called out in warning messages.

Safety & Laser
Concepts
The VJ 3340/3140 /3640 CO2 laser is a 2 box design (Marking Unit and Supply Unit, connected by flexible umbilical containing
all electrical connections between Marking Head and Supply Unit).
l Available beam sources:
l 10W CO2 laser for VJ3140
l 30W CO2 laser for VJ3340
l 60W CO2 laser for VJ3640
l Available marking heads:
l SHC60
l SHC100
l SHC120
l SHC150 (VJ3340 and VJ3640 only).
l Also comes in an IP65 version (wash down).
The safety guidelines provided in this lesson are intended to educate those who attend the technical training on all safety issues
in order to install, program, operate, and maintain the printer in a safe manner.
Lesson Objectives
l Determine what are suitable safety glasses for working with CO2 lasers
l Define fire hazards associated with VJ 3340/3140 /3640 CO2 lasers
l Define the electrical hazards associated with VJ 3340/3140 /3640  CO2 lasers
l Explain the dangers of fume hazards and how they can be prevented
l Identify how to properly install beam shields, guards and interlocks
l Identify general safety instructions and procedures
l Define the different laser classifications
Lesson Activities
In this section, you will review the safety conventions to be used throughout the training. You will monitor one another’s adher -
ence to the safety guidelines and note when not in compliance. Students not in compliance with the safety guidelines will be
expected to comply upon notification.

3140 3340 3640 Technician Training
What is a Laser?
A laser is "a device that produces radiant energy predominantly by stimulated emission. Laser radiation may be highly coherent
temporally, or spatially, or both" (ANSI Z136.1 - 2014).
The term "Laser" was originally coined as an acronym for L ight A mplified by S timulated E mission of R adiation.
Characteristics of a Laser Beam
l Monochromatic
l Light is of a single color or wave length
l CO2 laser light is invisible to the human eye
l Collimated
l Laser light is "parallel" and has little divergence or spreading
l Coherent
l All light energy particles or photons travel in phase with each other
F IGURE 1- 1: An example of the monochromatic wavelength of laser light.
Product Marking Techniques
Unlike an ink or pigment based coders that apply a new material onto a substrate, lasers mark on products by altering the sub -
strate itself.
14
Rev AF

Safety & Laser Concepts
Ablation
Ablation is the removal of a layer to reveal contrasting layer below. The topmost layer of film or paint on the substrate is vapor -
ized.
F IGURE 1- 2: A surface coating ablated off of a substrate.
Thermal or Chemical Change
In a thermal/chemical change technique, light and heat from the beam interact with the substrate to alter it at the surface
without changing its texture or removing substrate. On metals this is occurs as a function of surface oxides forming on the sub -
strate surface at the point of beam contact. Similarly on plastics, the beam spurs molecules of the substrate to react and change
color. If the plastic does not react, dyes or additives may be added to the plastic medium before formation. The beam will react
with those additives during marking.
15
Rev AF

3140 3340 3640 Technician Training
F IGURE 1- 3: Substrates altered by thermal/chemical reactions at the surface.
Physical Change - Surface Modification
Lasers may mark on substrates by burning away substrate to a target depth (in the tens of microns). A trench is left behind in the
shape of the marked characters or graphics. Substrates that are too thin for engraving (e.g. plastic bottles) may simply be mod -
ified at the surface causing the formation of bubbles that raise up rather than leave behind a trench. Glass does not burn and is
marked by crazing , or shattering, at the point of beam contact.
Marks in Glass Substrates Sharpen Over Time
Since marks on glass substrates rely on crazing and not vaporization, the removed substrate does not always
immediately fall away. This means that at first a mark on the glass substrate may appear faint but will
sharpen as the substrate jostles on a conveyor and the tiny shards fall away.
Engraving
When engraving, a superficial incision removes a few microns of material. This occurs via melting/vaporization (metal), partial
burning (thermoplastic), or crazing (glass).
16
Rev AF

Safety & Laser Concepts
F IGURE 1- 4: Surfaces marked via engraving.
Substrate Modification
When marking by substrate modification, the beam causes an additive effect on the substrate rather than a reductive one as
occurs in engraving. The surface is momentarily softened and then resolidifies. While softened, impurities such as oxides or
gasses are released that alter the color or texture of the substrate.
17
Rev AF

3140 3340 3640 Technician Training
F IGURE 1- 5: Substrates marked via surface modification.
How Does a Laser Work?
Laser beams are the product of energy discharged from atoms as photons. This energy emits as electrons orbiting the nucleus of
the atoms decay to a lower energy level. The energy that formerly kept that electron moving at a higher orbit converts to a
photon.
Emissions may be spontaneous or stimulated . Spontaneous emissions occur naturally based on the interaction of excited
atoms in the electromagnetic field in their environment but are incoherent. Stimulated emissions occur when an incoming
photon of a specific frequency interacts with an excited atomic electron causing it to decay to a lower energy level. This inter -
action is called absorption .
Ordinarily absorption produces no photons as the electrons simply raise to a higher energy level. Lasers work because the envir -
onment inside the beams source is such that instead of raising to a higher energy level, electrons instead decay and the dif -
ference in energy is released as a second photon of the same phase, frequency, polarization, and direction of travel.
18
Rev AF

Safety & Laser Concepts
F IGURE 1- 6: Illustration of Spontaneous Emission. The F IGURE 1- 7: Illustration of Absorption. An incoming
orbit of the electron decays and a photon photon is absorbed and the increase in
escapes. energy results in an electron rising to a
higher orbit.
F IGURE 1- 8: Illustration of the interaction of light and matter that occurs in stimulated emission. A single photon strikes a higher
orbit electron which drops to a lower orbit. The original photon and a second photon are emitted.
Stimulated Emission & Light Amplification
Energy is applied to the laser beam source to stimulate emission.
19
Rev AF

3140 3340 3640 Technician Training
F IGURE 1- 9: Illustration of activity inside of a laser beam source (i.e. laser tube).
As photons move through the gain medium an avalanche effect occurs as collisions with electrons produce more and more
photons, all cohering and moving on together in same direction at the same wavelength.
F IGURE 1- 10: An illustration of the amplification or "avalanche" effect on photons in a gain medium.
20
Rev AF

Safety & Laser Concepts
Laser Wavelengths
Laser beams, being light and therefore a form of electromagnetic radiation, can be produced at varying wavelengths on the
EM spectrum .
Wavelength is the distance between one peak or crest of a light wave and the next peak or crest. It is equal
to the speed of the wave divided by its frequency, and to the speed of a wave times its period.
Electromagnetic radiation has varying effects on people and things. The radio waves, light waves, and X Rays are all
EM radiation. Radio Waves are harmless to people, light is dangerous only at some exposures and wavelengths, and X Rays are
dangerous at more than minimal occasional exposure.
F IGURE 1- 11: Where laser radiation wavelengths fall on the electromagnetic spectrum.
The primary wavelengths of laser radiation for current commercial applications fall within the Ultraviolet (UV), visible, and
infrared ranges of the spectrum. UV laser radiation wavelengths range between 180 and 400 nm. Visible light wavelengths range
between 400 and 700 nm. The infrared range of the EM spectrum consists of radiation at wavelengths between 700 nm and 1
mm.
21
Rev AF

3140 3340 3640 Technician Training
Effects of Laser Radiation on Biological Tissue
Laser radiation absorbed by the skin penetrates only a few layers. In the eye, visible and near infrared radiation passes through
the cornea and is focused on and absorbed by the retina. It is the wavelength of the light that determines the visible sensation of
color: violet at 400 nm, red at 700 nm, and the other colors of the visible spectrum in between. When radiation is absorbed by bio -
logical tissue, the effect on that tissue is either photochemical , thermal , or mechanical .
l In the ultraviolet range the effect is primarily photochemical.
l In the infrared range the effect is primarily thermal.
l In the visible range both effects are present.
l When the intensity of the radiation is sufficiently high, the result will be damage to the absorbing tissue.

Danger to Eyes & Skin
l The laser system produces class 4 laser radiation.
l Infrared laser radiation is not visible for the human eye. Therefore eyes and skin are endangered through high- energy laser
radiation.
l High radiation intensity causes a local heating and burning of the cornea and thus a reduction or loss of sight!
22
Rev AF

Safety & Laser Concepts
Take appropriate measures to protect people working near the laser against beam exposure!
All personnel within the room must wear safety goggles suitable against laser radiation while performing main -
tenance or adjustments at the open laser housing or near an open beam delivery system!
Never look directly into the laser beam!
The skin can withstand higher radiation intensities than the eye. However, depending on the radiation exposure period and radi -
ation intensity, the radiation burns and destroys tissue.
Protect your skin by wearing adequate protective clothing! Avoid any exposure of your skin to laser radiation!
Do not allow the laser beam to contact your clothing!

The Complete Laser System
A complete and closed laser system acts as a class 1 laser system in normal operation. Laser radiation emitted by the laser source
is held completely within shielding until it reaches the product. The shielding prevents emergence of laser radiation or laser beam
reflection.
23
Rev AF

3140 3340 3640 Technician Training
Care of System Umbilical
The minimum bending radius of the umbilical cable is 40 mm. The umbilical is made for stationary use. It will
also sustain slow moving with low acceleration a few times a day.
System Overheat
Pay close attention to the laser's air flow. Failure to do so will result in damage to the laser source or over -
heating of the system boards.
l The laser system is cooled by air. The internal cooling system is designed to supply sufficient cooling.
l Make sure that the cooling air can be drawn in and blown out freely and that there is sufficient air exchange
at the installation site to ensure heat dissipation.
Radiation Exposure Hazard - Eye
F IGURE 1- 12: Illustrated visual impact of eye damage caused by laser radiation.
To understand the possible health effects, it is important to understand the functions of the major parts of the human eye.
The Human Eye
The cornea is the transparent layer of tissue covering the eye. Damage to the outer cornea may be uncomfortable (like a gritty
feeling) or painful, but will usually heal quickly. Damage to deeper layers of the cornea may cause permanent injury.
F IGURE 1- 13: Parts of the human eye.
24
Rev AF

Safety & Laser Concepts
The cornea is a clear, dome- shaped surface that covers the front of the eye. It is the first and most powerful lens in the eye's
optical system. To keep it transparent the cornea contains no blood vessels. Tears that flow over it and aqueous humor in the
chamber behind it keep it nourished. When you hear of eye transplants, it is the cornea that is being replaced. The cornea can be
damaged by accidents, infections, and genetic defects.
The pupil is the black circle in the center of the eye, and its primary function is to monitor the amount of light that comes into
the eye. When there is a lot of light, the pupil contracts to keep the light from overwhelming the eye. When there is very little
light, the pupil expands so it can soak up as much as possible.
The iris is the colored part of the eye. Although it might seem purely cosmetic, the iris actually functions to adjust the size of the
pupil. It has muscles that contract or expand depending on the amount of light the pupil needs to process images.
The lens exists behind the pupil and is responsible for allowing your eyes to focus on small details like words in a book. The lens is
in a constant state of adjustment as it becomes thinner or thicker to accommodate the detailed input it receives. With age, the
lens loses a lot of its elasticity which often results in cataracts and presbyopia, i.e. nearsightedness, because the lens cannot
adjust as well to its surroundings as it used to.
The vitreous humour is a gel- like substance that helps to keep the eyeball in its proper, circular shape. This is the area in the eye
where floaters develop as pieces of the vitreous humor clump together and cast shadows onto the retina. With age, the vitreous
humor begins to shrink and can cause problems like posterior retinal detachment or retinal tears.
The retina is the area at the back of the eye that receives the refined, visual message from the front of the eye, and it transmits
that visual message to the brain using electrical signals.
The sclera is the white part of the eye, and its main function is to provide strength, structure, and protection for the eye. The
sclera contains blood vessels that can tell an eye doctor a lot about the overall state of your health.
F IGURE 1- 14: Example of corneal damage.
Ocular Absorption by Wavelength
Damage to the eye is dependent upon the wavelength of the beam.
25
Rev AF

3140 3340 3640 Technician Training
F IGURE 1- 15: Ocular absorption by wavelength, illustrated.
l Ultraviolet and low energy far- infrared radiation can injure the epithelial layer of the cornea; a condition that is painful and
visually handicapping. At lower powers, this injury is primarily due to a photochemical reaction. A latency period of hours
may exist between the time of exposure and the development of the corneal pathology. Minimal corneal lesions heal within
a few days, but meanwhile hinder vision.
l High energy far- infrared radiation is mainly absorbed by the cornea producing immediate burns at all corneal layers. An
infrared laser can produce a burn resulting in immediate visual incapacitation and may lead to permanent corneal scarring.
Very high energy radiation can perforate the cornea which may result in complete loss of the eye.
l Laser light in the visible to near infrared spectrum (i.e., 400 - 1400 nm) can cause damage to the retina resulting in scotoma
(blind spot in the fovea). This wave band is also know as the "retinal hazard region". Exposure to the Q- switched Nd:YAG
laser beam (1064 nm) is especially hazardous and may initially go undetected because the beam is invisible and the retina
lacks pain sensory nerves. Photoacoustic retinal damage may be associated with an audible "pop" at the time of exposure.
Visual disorientation due to retinal damage may not be apparent to the operator until considerable thermal damage has
occurred.
Radiation Exposure Hazard - Skin
Skin is the largest organ of the body and, as such, is at the greatest risk for coming in contact with the laser beam. The head,
hands, and arms are the most likely skin surfaces to be exposed.
26
Rev AF

Safety & Laser Concepts
l Stratum Corneum (Dead Layer)
The stratum corneum is the outermost layer. It consists of flattened, dead epidermal cells that protect the living tissue from
water loss, injury from physical objects, and radiant energy. The thickness is 8- 20 μm except on the soles of the feet and the
palms of the hands where it is 500- 600 μm.
l Epidermis
The epidermis is the outermost layer of living tissue, where the tanning process takes place. It has a relatively uniform thick -
ness of about 50- 150 μm.
l Dermis Corium
The dermis corium is made up largely of connective tissue which gives the skin its elasticity and supportive strength.
Included in this layer are nerve cells, blood vessels, and lymphatic glands. The thickness of this layer varies over the body
from 1- 4 mm.
l Subcutaneous Tissue
The subcutaneous tissue is made up mostly of fatty tissue serving as insulation and as a shock absorption medium. The
thickness of this layer varies according to the area of the body as well as from person to person.
Dermal Damage by Radiation Varies by Wavelength
Laser effects on tissue depend on the power density of the incident beam, absorption of tissues at the incident wavelength, time
beam is held on tissue, and the effects of blood circulation and heat conduction in the affected area.
27
Rev AF

3140 3340 3640 Technician Training
F IGURE 1- 16: Effects of laser radiation by wavelength.
Immediate Effects of Exposure
The immediate effect of exposure to laser light above the biological damage threshold is normally tissue burns. Injury to the skin
can be caused by either temperature elevation in skin tissues or from a photochemical effect (e.g., "sunburn") from excessive
levels of actinic ultraviolet radiation.
28
Rev AF

Safety & Laser Concepts
F IGURE 1- 17: Examples of skin burns.
Delayed Effects of Exposure
The possibility of adverse effects from repeated or chronic laser irradiation to the skin has been suggested, although it is normally
discounted. Only optical radiation in the ultraviolet region of the spectrum has been shown to cause long- term, delayed effects
such as accelerated skin aging and skin cancer. At present, laser safety standards for exposure of the skin attempt to take these
adverse effects into account.
Burns, skin cancer and acceleration of skin aging are possible from exposure to laser radiation. Chronic exposure can cause
increased pigmentation.
Fire Hazards
Understand fire hazards, how to prevent them, and how to react to fires.
Elements of a Fire
l Oxygen
l Heat
l Fuel
Fire Hazard Terminology
l Flash Point
Minimum temperature at which a liquid will burn. The Flashpoint for Methyl Ethyl Keytone (MEK) 73°F.
29
Rev AF

3140 3340 3640 Technician Training
l Flammable Range
Environmental conditions in which there is a fuel/air mixture that leads to combustion, e.g not too lean (too little fuel), not
too rich (too much fuel).
l Lower Flammability Limit (LFL)
The lower limit of the flammable range. Also know as Lower Explosive Limit (LEL).
l Upper Flammability Limit (UFL)
The upper limit of the flammable range. Also known as Upper Explosive Limit (UEL).
l Bonding
Connecting two objects or pieces of equipment with a conductive cable to maintain the same electrical potential between
the two. Grounding
Classes of Fire
Fires are classified as one of four types.
Class A - Ordinary Combustibles
Most common fire. A burning house is a class A fire.
Class B - Flammable Liquids
Liquid combustibles such as paint strippers, solvents, fuels, oil, or grease.
Class C - Electrical Equipment
Additional danger from electrical shock. Power equipment OFF immediately.
Class D - Combustible Metals
Combustible metals such as magnesium, aluminum, and metallic sodium. Metals burn easier in powder or chip form.
DO NOT apply water to a Class D fire as this can result in an explosion.
Fire Prevention & Safety
l Always store flammable liquids in approved cabinets.
l Never operate the laser system on unattended material.
l Always keep the area around the machine clean and free of clutter, combustible materials, explosives, or volatile solvents
such as acetone, alcohol, or gasoline.
l Always keep a properly maintained and inspected fire extinguisher on hand. A Halotron fire extinguisher or a multi- purpose
dry- chemical fire extinguisher. The Halotron extinguisher emits a clean, easily removable substance that is not harmful to
the mechanics or wiring of the laser system. Other dry chemical extinguisher can sometimes emit a sticky, corrosive powder
that is very difficult to clean up.
l Always use a fume extractor when vector printing.
l Many materials have the potential to burst suddenly into flames – even materials with which you might be very familiar.
Utilizing the Air Assist feature will reduce the instance of flare- ups when working with flammable materials such as acrylic
and wood.
l Keep your laser system clean - Build up of cutting and engraving residue and debris is dangerous and can create a fire
hazard in its own right. These small pieces present a very dangerous fire hazard, especially if they are allowed to collect over
time. Since most users cut wood and acrylic, these small pieces that fall into the conveyor, extractor nozzle or build up
around the lens act just like kindling and can ignite and start a fire.

are 99% nuisance dust.

Fume Exposure First Aid
Symptoms of exposure to this product are headaches, dizziness, nausea, difficult breathing, vomiting and diarrhea. In all cases of
overexposure get medical attention immediately. Take victim to a doctor or emergency treatment facility.

l Move person to fresh air.

mouth, if possible.
l Keep warm and make sure person can breathe freely.
l Call a poison control center or doctor for further treatment advice.

In Case of Ingestion
l Call a poison control center or doctor immediately for treatment advice.
l Have person drink one or two glasses of water and induce vomiting by touching back of throat with finger, or
if available, administer syrup of ipecac.
l Do not give anything by mouth to an unconscious person.
l If on skin or clothing:
l Brush or shake material off clothes and shoes in a well- ventilated area. Allow clothes to aerate in a ventilated
area prior to laundering.
l Do not leave contaminated clothing in occupied and/or confined areas such as automobiles, vans, motel
rooms, etc.
l Wash contaminated skin thoroughly with soap and water.
In Case of Eye Exposure
l Hold eye open and rinse slowly and gently with water for 15- 20 minutes.
l Remove contact lenses, if present, after the first 5 minutes, then continue rinsing eye.
l Call a poison control center or doctor for treatment advice.

Zinc Selenide (ZnSe) - transparent in wide spectral range from yellow (visible) to far IR. ZnSe material is a chemically inert, non-

ZnSe (Zinc Selenide) Chemical Vapor Deposition (CVD) material is produced by synthesis from zinc vapour and H2Se gas, form -

ture; the grain size of Zinc Selenide CVD material is controlled to produce maximum strength and machinability.

Laser Accident Prevention
Statistically, laser accidents are due to several factors. The most common type of injury is to the eye (around 70%). There have
been a few deaths, mostly caused by electrocution or fires.
Causes of Laser Accidents
l Eye exposure unexpectedly during alignment
l No eye protection worn
l Equipment malfunction
l High voltage mishandling
l Non- beam hazards
l Incorrect eyewear
l Inhalation of air contaminants
l Viewing laser- generated plasmas
l General incorrect use of equipment
Laser Classification
A laser's class is an indication of the beam hazard level of that laser or laser system during normal operation or the determination
thereof. The hazard level of a laser or laser system is represented by a number or a numbered capital letter. The laser clas -
sifications are Class 1, Class 1M, Class 2, Class 2M, Class 3R, Class 3B, and Class 4. (ANSI Z136.1- 2014)

Laser Class 1
l No danger to the eyes (also with the use of optical instruments).
l No danger to the skin.
l The accessible laser radiation is, under reasonably foreseeable conditions, harmless.
The products are absolutely laser- light safe (eye & skin): no laser light at critical power levels can leave the laser system. This is
the standard for laser marking machines. Laser Class 1 products are operated in the same manner as conventional machine tools.
Perhaps the most common form of a Class I laser system is a high- power laser that has been totally enclosed (embedded) inside
a protective enclosure equipped with appropriate interlocks and/or labels on all removable panels or access doors. Beam access is
prevented, therefore, during operation and maintenance.
F IGURE 1- 21: A Class 1 Laser with extraction and carbon block.
The accessible laser radiation is harmless for the eye as long as the beam diameter is not reduced by optical components (e.g.
binoculars, demagnification glasses). As long as no optical focusing components are used the risk assessment is not higher than
in laser class 1.
Caution
When optical components are used, the risk assessment can be comparable with laser class 3R or 3B.
Laser Class 2
l No danger to the eyes at short exposure times (max. 0.25 s).
l No danger to the skin.
l The accessible laser radiation is only in the visible range (400 to 700 nm).
Short irradiation (up to 0.25 s) is not dangerous for the eye. The eye protects itself via the eyelid- closing reflex. For CW lasers of
this class the maximum accessible power level is 1 mW.

3140 3340 3640 Technician Training
High power lasers (cw: 500 mW or greater, pulsed: 10 J/cm2 or the diffuse reflection limit) are hazardous to view under any con -
dition (directly or diffusely scattered) and are a potential fire hazard, LGAC hazard, and a skin hazard. Significant controls are
required of Class 4 laser facilities.
Interaction of Light & Matter
Most absorbing materials satisfy the Beer- Lambert Law.
An isolated atom scatters light because the electric field of the incident light wave forces the electrons in the atom to oscillate
back and forth about their equilibrium position. By the laws of electromagnetism, when a charge changes its velocity, it emits
radiation.
Light is emitted uniformly in all directions in the plane ∞ to oscillation, but decreases in amplitude as the viewing angle shifts
away from that plane.
l Reflection occurs when light is scattered in the opposite direction of incident light.
l Refraction is a phenomenon in which light is scattered in the forward direction and combines with the incident beam. The
physical effect of this combination is to make the transmitted light appear as though it has traveled more slowly through
the sample than through a vacuum.
l Incident ray in physics, a ray of light which hits a surface.
l The refracted ray or transmitted ray corresponding to a given incident ray represents the light that is transmitted
through the surface. The angle between this ray and the normal is known as the angle of refraction, and it is given by Snell's
40
Rev AF

Safety & Laser Concepts
Law. Conservation of energy requires that the power in the incident ray must equal the sum of the power in the transmitted
ray, the power in the reflected ray, and any power absorbed at the surface.
Reflection of Light
In the case of free propagation of a laser beam, special attention should be placed as the vision of the radiation is directly reflec -
ted, because of exposure to the eye may be exposed. Less dangerous is the vision of widespread radiation.
Intrabeam exposure means that the eye or skin is exposed directly to all or part of the laser beam. The eye or skin is exposed to
the full irradiance or radiant exposure possible.
Reflection is the return of radiant energy (incident light) by a surface, with no change in wavelength.
Diffuse Reflection takes place when different parts of a beam incident on a surface are reflected over a wide range of angles in
accordance with Lambert's Law. The intensity will fall off as the inverse of the square of the distance away from the surface and
also obey a Cosine Law of reflection.
Note
Direct (intrabeam) veiwing is the most dangerous of the three types.
41
Rev AF

3140 3340 3640 Technician Training
Specular vs Diffuse Reflection
F IGURE 1- 22: The difference between light reflected on different surfaces.
Reflection off of smooth surfaces such as mirrors or a calm body of water leads to a type of reflection known as specular reflec -
tion . Reflection off of rough surfaces such as clothing, paper, and the asphalt roadway leads to a type of reflection known as dif -
fuse reflection . Whether the surface is microscopically rough or smooth has a tremendous impact upon the subsequent
reflection of a beam of light. The diagram below depicts two beams of light incident upon a rough and a smooth surface.
A light beam can be thought of as a bundle of individual light rays which are traveling parallel to each other. Each individual light
ray of the bundle follows the law of reflection. If the bundle of light rays is incident upon a smooth surface, then the light rays
reflect and remain concentrated in a bundle upon leaving the surface. On the other hand, if the surface is microscopically rough,
the light rays will reflect and diffuse in many different directions.
Specular reflections are mirror- like reflections and can reflect close to 100% of the incident light. Flat surfaces will not change a
fixed beam diameter, only the direction. Convex surfaces will cause beam spreading, and concave surfaces will make the beam
converge.
Diffuse reflections result when surface irregularities scatter light in all directions. The specular nature of a surface is dependent
upon the wavelength of incident radiation. A specular surface is one that has a surface roughness less than the wavelength of
the incident light. A very rough surface is not specular to visible light but might be to IR radiation of 10.6 µm from a CO2 laser.
Why a rough Surface Diffuses a Light Beam
For each type of reflection, each individual ray follows the law of reflection. However, the roughness of the material means that
each individual ray meets a surface which has a different orientation. The normal line at the point of incidence is different for dif -
ferent rays. Subsequently, when the individual rays reflect off the rough surface according to the law of reflection, they scatter in
different directions. The result is that the rays of light are incident upon the surface in a concentrated bundle and are diffused
upon reflection.
Counter Measures
l Make sure that the working distance is set correctly before marking any reflecting material. Use a weakly reflecting material
for testing, e.g. laminated paper.
l When marking reflecting material try not to mark in the center of the marking field. Position the product so that the mark -
ing is in the edge of the marking field.
l When setting the laser power start with the maximum value and reduce the power until the marking result is satisfying. If
no marking is visible at maximum power the material cannot be marked using this laser.

ANSI Z 136.1 - 2014
The Laser Institute of America (LIA) has been the secretariat and publisher for this series of standards for the safe use of lasers
since 1986. Z136.1 is the parent document, other specialized
standards exist.
Purpose
l Create a standard of measurement.
l Determine thresholds where radiant energy becomes a hazard.
l Categorizes laser into four basic hazards.
l Provides recommendations for the safe usage of lasers and
laser systems between 180nm and 1mm.

General Laser Safety
The laser system must only be used for the treatment of material surfaces. The surfaces are locally heated by intensive class 4
laser radiation and are modified thereby. These systems are mainly applied for marking of product surfaces (expiry dates, batch
printing, serial numbers, etc.).
The radiation produced by the laser system is of high energy and therefore improper use represents a danger to persons or
objects!
Never expose human beings or animals to laser radiation.
This might result in severe burns of eyes or skin.
Never expose flammable materials to laser radiation!
Ensure the laser beam is housed in a shielded enclosure. Paper (circuit diagrams, leaflets, posters on the walls,
etc.), curtains not treated with flame retardant, wooden boards, or similar flammable materials can easily
ignite by direct or reflected laser radiation. Install smoke detectors, fire detectors, or similar or similar safety
equipment to detect fire should it occur.
Never expose reflective surfaces to laser radiation!
Never expose reflective surfaces to laser radiation! The reflected laser beam may cause the same dangers - in
some cases even greater dangers - as the original laser beam.
Never expose unknown materials to laser radiation!
Some materials (e.g. polyethylene, polypropylene, glass) are easily penetrated by the laser beam, although
they seem to be opaque for the human eye.
Danger of explosion! Make sure that the laser area is free of flam -
mable/explosive materials and vapors!.
Arbitrary modifications or changes to the laser system are forbidden.
If a modification made by the user to a previously classified laser system leads to a change of its performance
data and/or its intended use that person or organization performing the modification is responsible for a new
classification and labeling of the laser system. The person or organization is then regarded as the man -
ufacturer .
The laser system must be operated with open laser and/or open
beam delivery system by specially trained personnel only!
Observe all laser protection rules.
Feedback Radiation
When marking highly reflective material there might be a feedback of radiation into the laser. In this case the
system switches to fatal error mode.
A reset has to be carried out to continue: the laser must be switched off and on again.

Both polycarbonate and PMMA  are highly suitable for constructing a laser beam protection for a CO2 laser system. Each has a
very good warning function. If exposed to too high radiation it emits smoke and soot as well as a strong smell. The staff can
notice the accident and take immediate action to prevent the fire from spreading. Since it can only absorb very low energy dens -
ities the following requirement must be met:
It must be guaranteed that in case of an error, if it misses the product to be marked or if it is emitted and there is no product to
be marked, the laser beam is not focused and not specularly reflected onto the polycarbonate or PMMA.
As a solution, the laser beam protection can be made of polycarbonate or PMMA if the side opposite to the opening from which
the laser beam is emitted or parts of the beam protection where problems due to specular reflection might occur are made of
something reflective, such as aluminum.
Polycarbonate and PMMA are proven for the construction of CO2 beam protections. The thickness of the material should be at
least 5 mm and depends decisively on the required stability of the protection and on the irradiation period expected. Depending
on the power class and the focal length of the focusing lens of the laser beam source determined minimum distances to the focal
plane must be kept.

reach areas by reflection that it would never have reached in the normal beam path. Due to focusing the energy density of these
reflections can be much higher than of the original beam!

Automatically Moving Products
If a moving product is stopped to be marked (marking at standstill) the marking area should be screened for every marking by an
automatically moving laser beam protection. The beam protection must cover the surrounding contours of the marking area com -
pletely so that the entire marking area is screened light- tight towards the outside. That means there must be no gaps. That
applies also to the side facing the opening from which the laser beam is emitted. In case of malfunction where the marking pro -
cess is started and there is no product in the marking area, laser radiation must not exit the beam protection at any side.
If the product to be marked is moving during the marking process, e.g. on a conveyer belt, the construction of a beam protection
tunnel is necessary. In order to control the transmission of laser radiation, the beam- emergence opening is encased together with
the conveyer belt from above and from the sides. If the radiation might penetrate the conveyer belt, e.g. at gaps, the tunnel
must also be installed from below. The tunnel must be long enough in front of and behind the marking area to prevent possible
reflections coming from the product itself, or other surfaces (e.g. metallic jointed- band conveyer), from exiting at the sides of the
tunnel. The tunnel must be long enough to absorb reflections before such reflections reach the ends of the tunnel. The tunnel
should be constructed to be as close as possible to the product. Thus potential exits for laser radiation are minimized. If possible
flaps, brushes or strip curtains should be installed at the ends of the tunnel to provide additional screening.
Personal Protection Equipment (PPE)
PPE control measures for working with lasers included glasses, face shields, gloves, and long sleeve clothing. Appropriate eye pro -
tection is always of utmost importance.
l Appropriate eyewear
l Must be appropriate for the laser wavelength in use to adequately attenuate the beam to safe levels
l Should be comfortable enough to wear
l Skin PPE (UV Only)
l Gloves
l Special Clothing (long sleeves)

Protective eyewear may be constructed with either glass or plastic (polycarbonate) lenses.

l Laser wavelength or range
l Optical Density (OD) for that wavelength or range (See "Optical Density of Protective Eyewear" on the next page )
l Damage Threshold of the eyewear (the maximum irradiance or beam power that the eyewear will protect against for at
least 5 seconds
l Visual transmittance of the eyewear (how much visible light is transmitted to the eye)
l Field of view and curvature of the lens
l Goggles large enough to accommodate prescription eyewear
l Ventilation to prevent fogging and general comfort
l Effect on color vision
l Impact resistance and cost
Eyewear Checklist
Is the OD for the worst- case situation?
Have different frame types been evaluated?
Have all wavelengths been determined?
Has visual transmittance been considered?
Can users be relied on to actually use the eyewear?
Will eyewear be available for guests?
49
Rev AF

3140 3340 3640 Technician Training
Optical Density of Protective Eyewear
When selecting protective eye wear for working near lasers, one must select glasses or goggles with Optical Density (OD) appro -
priate to the wavelength of the laser in use. OSHA defines OD as "a logarithmic expression for the attenuation produced by an
attenuating medium, such as an eye protection filter." Stated more mathematically, the ANSI Z136.1 document for the Safe Use
of Lasers defines OD as "The logarithm to the base ten of the reciprocal of the transmittance at a particular wavelength ."
How OD is Calculated
We will demonstrate the methodology behind calculating OD. However, if you are unsure what safety equipment to use consult
your Laser Safety Officer (LSO).
OD Level 6 Required
All Videojet Technologies, INC employees working with lasers shall wear glasses with an OD of 6 at the
wavelength in use.
There are several variable symbols used in the OD calculations and prerequisite calculations.
Value Symbol Calculated As
Optical Density Dƛ
Transmittance (Overexposure) τƛ
Exposure H
p
Maximum Permissible Exposure MPE Refer to ANSI Z136.1 - 2014 Table 5a
The MPE determination is based on the duration of use, and particular wavelength.
l Aversion response to visible lasers (0.4 to 0.7 um, and class 3B and 4 lasers). The aversion response time of 0.25 s shall be
used when long term intrabeam viewing is not intended.
l Near Infrared (0.7 to 1.4 um, and class 3B and 4 lasers). 10 s shall be used as a “worst case” time duration for MPE determ -
ination for this category of lasers.
l Diffuse Viewing (intended class 3B and 4). Maximum viewing times may be used within an 8 hour time period based on the
operation, such as with alignment procedures where viewing is intended.
l Diffuse Viewing (unintended class 3B and 4). 600 seconds shall be used for this category of laser classes where there is
potential for intense staring during an operation.
l Daily Occupational Exposure (long term, class 3B and 4). 30,000 s shall be used for long term 8 hour exposure durations to
low level light where applicable.
50

Safety & Laser Concepts
OD Calculation Example
An example OD calculation for a direct 100 watt CO laser beam. While no Videojet Technologies, INC C0 laser generally oper -
2 2
ates at this wattage, for safety purposes we use the highest possible wattage with an additional margin added to ensure all pos -
sible wattages are covered.
Optical Density = Log (10400) = 4.017
10
Round up to an OD of 5.
OD Level 6 Required
All Videojet Technologies, INC employees working with lasers shall wear glasses with an OD of 6 at the
wavelength in use.
Nominal Hazard Zone
Nominal Hazard Zone (NHZ) is a distance within which the irradiance of a beam is greater than the MPE. Besides being specific
to a given wavelength and time of exposure, a different NHZ can be defined for the beam’s path to your eye (direct viewing),
specular reflectance, and diffuse reflectance.
The NHZ is a practical definition; it has a specific shape around your particular laboratory apparatus. For instance, assuming your
lab has no windows and a solid door, the NHZ will in the worst case scenario be the area (e.g. square footage) of the lab itself. In
other words, the NHZ for you will be derived at the end of your safety calculations, and thereafter will be most useful to you for
planning control measures in you laboratory.
Nominal Hazard Zone Example Calculation
An example NHZ calculation for a 100 watt CO laser beam.

4. Press x or * to multiply.

Keypad & Key Switch
The membrane keypad is located on the front of the Supply Unit. Use this keypad to power the system ON, power the system
OFF, or start/stop marking,. LED lights on the keypad indicate the state of the laser system.
The keypad is part of the safety and power circuitry and must remain connected to the Chameleon board to keep power on to
the rest of the system. Disconnection will immediately shut down power.
F IGURE 2- 1: Supply unit keypad and key switch - front view.
Keypad Buttons
Three buttons are available on the keypad.
l Power/Standby - Press to boot the laser system or put an already booted laser into standby mode.
Current Potential in Standby Mode
When the laser is in Standby mode there is still power to the laser system as a whole. The only way to
completely shut down power is the remove power cord from outlet.
l Start - Press to start marking. The system will wait for a trigger if so configured in Product Registration/Line Setup.
l Stop - Immediately stop marking.
58
Rev AF

System Overview
Keypad LEDs
The lights on the membrane keypad indicate the status of the laser.
Green - Shutter State
l On when the shutter is closed, off when the shutter is open.
l Shutter will be open during the marking process and closed when Stop button is pressed.
Red - Emission
l On when laser power is active and there is potential for laser beam emission.
l A redundant LED on the marking head ensures that anyone can see the red light whether looking at Supply Unit or Marking
Head.
Yellow - Error
l On indicates an Error condition.
l You must correct and confirm the error in the user interface before operation can continue.
Keypad Key Switch
The key switch toggles power to the laser tube. Remove the key when the laser is not in use to prevent unauthorized operation.
Laser power will not be enabled unless the key switch is closed.
An external/remote key switch may be connected to the unit through the Customer Interface section. (See "X33 - Internal Con -
nections" on page 202 ).

Supply Unit Interior
The Supply Unit is the part of the system most responsible for processing, power generation, and power distribution.
Components in the Supply Cabinet
l Chameleon board - main circuit board for system
l 48 Volt Power Supply - power for electronics and marking head
l 36 or 48 Volt Laser Power Supply (3140/3340 only) - power for laser tube only (specific power supply depends on laser tube
version). Different 48V laser power supply than the 3640.
l 48 Volt Laser Power Supply (3640 only) - power for laser tube only. Different 48V laser power supply than 3140/3340.
l HIC - Head Interface Controller - adapts the Chameleon board to CO2 marking heads
l LIC_ CO2 - adapts the Chameleon board to supported CO2 laser types
l CPD3 - safety control board used in older model 3140/3340 and in conjunction with FASS in newer model 3140/3340 when
FASS is needed
l SPM - safety control board in newer model 3140/3340 only
l SPM- 16A or SPM- 16A- FASS board - safety control board (3640 only)
l FASS - optional safety control board to be used in combination with CPD3 in all 3140/3340 lasers where performance level
rating is required

Power Supply - Electronics 48V
Two power supplies drive the 3340/3140 /3640 : one power supply for the laser tube and a 48 volt power supply for all other elec -
tronics in the unit.
The 48 Volt power supply for the electronics drives the:
l Chameleon board
l CP- Light board
l Galvos
The only component not supplied with power by the 48V electronics power supply is the laser tube. The 48V electronics power
supply used in older 3140/3340/3640 lasers was a 240 Watt (SP 240- 48) power supply. Lasers manufactured since 2019 use a
48V 200 Watt (UHP 200- 48) electronics power supply. The UHP 200- 48 is now the standard spare part and is backwards com -
patible to replace the 240 Watt version, if needed.
75
Rev AF

3140 3340 3640 Technician Training
Note Different 48V Power Supplies when Ordering and Installing
There are up to three different 48V power supplies used in 3140/3340/3640 lasers depending on when they
were manufactured. The 48V supply for electronics should not be confused with 48V supply for laser
tubes on newer 3340 and all 3640 lasers as the Wattage is extremely different. Always verify that you are
ordering correct power supply.
Location
The 48 Volt power supply is located on the underside of the mounting plate beneath the Chameleon board in the supply unit. To
remove or replace the power supply, you must remove the mounting plate chassis to which all components inside the supply unit
are mounted.
F IGURE 3- 4: Top view of supply unit interior with arrow to 48V power supply.
Figure 3- 5 on the facing page shows the relative location of the electronics power supply on 3140/3340 laser systems. The actual
size and model of power supply may vary by generation, but the relative location does not change.

3140/3340 Laser Power Supply
The 3140/3340 have a power supply dedicated only to providing DC voltage to the laser tube and fan for laser tube (IP54 only).
This power supply does not power any other component in the laser system. In all 3140 lasers and in 3340 lasers with a V30 laser
tube this laser power supply is 36 Volt/630 Watt. In new 3340 lasers with a Vi30 laser tube, the laser power supply is 48 Volt/624
Watt.
Both the 36V and 48V versions of the laser power supply must be adjusted based on the length of umbilical on the laser if the
laser power supply is replaced. See "Adjusting Laser Power Supply Voltage" on page 373 for instructions and tolerances for adjust -
ing voltage output of laser power supply.
Location
The laser power supply is located inside the supply unit on the underside of the mounting plate beneath the terminal block. To
remove or replace the power supply, you must remove the mounting plate chassis to which all components inside the supply unit
are mounted.

3640 Laser Power Supply (48V)
The laser tube in the 3640 laser system is powered by a 48V/2000 Watt power supply. This power supply is only used to power
the tube and nothing else in the system. The 48V supply is adjusted by manufacturing for both new laser units and also for spare
power supplies to 50V. The laser power supply should always stay adjusted to 50V and is not changed based on length of umbil -
ical.
There is another 48V/240 Watt power supply that is used to power all other components in the 3640 laser system and care
should be taken when ordering replacement parts to ensure correct 48V supply is ordered.
Location
The 48V Volt power supply is located inside the supply unit on the underside of the mounting plate beneath the Chameleon
board. To remove or replace the power supply you must remove the mounting plate chassis to which all components inside the
supply unit are mounted.

Terminal Block (X200)
The terminal block in the supply unit acts as a junction for voltage and signals created in the supply unit. These are sent out to
the laser tube, CP- Light, supply unit fan, and some optional interfaces.
Location
The terminal block is located inside the supply unit, to the left of the Chameleon board.
F IGURE 3- 15: Terminal block location.
Connections
The terminal block is the junction for 24V to the marking head and user interface from the Chameleon board as well as the
Voltage for the 3140/3340/3640 laser tube . The Input side contains the input from the laser power supply as well as 24V input
from the Chameleon board. The Output side sends power and ground to the laser tube and 24V and ground to the cooling fan
for supply unit, G55 handheld, or TouchUS PC (TU420/TU420H) where these are installed. The 3640 model also has a 48V and
GND for supplying the CP- Light board.

3140 3340 3640 Technician Training
Chameleon Board
The Chameleon board is the main controller board for the 3340/3140 /3640 laser system and is located within the supply unit of
the laser system.
There are three versions of the Chameleon board: Single Core, Multi- Core, and Multi- Core Industrial Protocols. It is very important
to know which board is in use as both the method of loading the operating system and available user interfaces differ between
these three versions of the board.
90
Rev AF

Supply Unit Interior
Main Components
F IGURE 3- 18: Major components of Chameleon Multi- Core Industrial Protocols labeled.
QSeven Module (Q7)
The QSeven module is the communication gateway for:
l Ethernet
l Serial port
l SD card
l EEPROM
l USB
The QSeven Module acts as an interface between Chameleon and Customer Interface.
The module stores the SVG database and contains a 1.46 GHz Intel ATOM processor with 1 GB RAM for Single Core Chameleon
boards. Multi- Core Chameleon boards have an upgraded QSeven module with 2 GB of RAM and 8 GB of Flash memory.
You can distinguish the two boards by looking at the Q7 module and board color. A Q7 with silver pins on the heat sink denotes a
Single Core Chameleon while a Q7 with a black linear heat sink (fins) is present on both the Multi- Core Chameleon board and
Multi- Core Industrial Protocols board. The main board of the Multi- Core Industrial Protocols Chameleon is additionally black in
color and also has a connector for the optional MIC board between the Q7 and the dip switches. This connector is not present on
the green Multi- Core board.
91
Rev AF

3140 3340 3640 Technician Training
F IGURE 3- 19: Q7 circled. (Left) A Single Core Chameleon. (Center) A Multi- Core Chameleon. (Right) A Multi- Core Industrial Pro -
tocols Chameleon.
Digital Signal Processor (DSP)
The DSP controls:
l Laser source control
l Marking trigger process
l Marking head control
l Marking process
Field Programmable Gate Array (FPGA)
The FPGA interface sends received information to the QSeven and DSP .
The FPGA receives from:
l Marking Heads (HIC)
l Laser Source (LIC)
l PCI Express
l SRAM
l CAN
l Customer Interface
Put simply, everything feeds into the FPGA and then goes out to either the QSeven or DSP.
92
Rev AF

Supply Unit Interior
EEPROM
The EEPROM is the nonvolatile memory that stores the system database. The system database includes:
l Laser type
l Head type
l Lens type
l Configuration data
l Serial number
l IP addresses
SD Card
The SD card is the boot drive for the system and houses both the Linux AOS operating system and CMark software . The SD
card used must be industrial- grade and have a minimum of 1 GB capacity.
The SD Card also stores:
l Customer database (Templates, Fonts, Graphics, etc.)
l Component files (Laser, Marking Head, Lens)
l Processor and board software (CP- Light, FPGA, and Chameleon board)
The SD card slot is present on both Single Core and Multi- Core Chameleon boards but is only used on Single Core Boards. Multi-
Core and Multi- Core Industrial Protocols Chameleon boards store the operating system in Flash memory present in the QSeven
module.
Battery
A 3V Lithium CR2032 battery located on the Chameleon board powers the memory to retain dynamic data stored in the SRAM
while the laser system is powered down. This dynamic data includes:
l Date and time (clock runs even if switched off)
l Dynamic data for Templates (serial numbers, variable text, shift codes, etc.)
l Last Template used
93
Rev AF

3140 3340 3640 Technician Training
The life of the battery is dependent on the amount of time the system is completely powered off. Typical battery life with system
power on (in Standby mode or On) is 3 years. If the system is completely powered down, the life is more than 1 year.
The laser will not boot without the battery in place or with a dead battery. Symptoms of a dead battery are the white light on
solid on start up and that the white light never begins to flash.
Dip Switches
There is a set of four DIP switches on the control board. DIP switch 1 is usable as needed, but leave all other switches (2- 4) set to
the OFF position.
Use DIP Switch 1 to Set the Laser IP Address
You must cycle power on the laser system after moving a DIP switch for changes to take effect.
l ON : At boot. the TCP/IP settings from the system database are ignored and the default IP and subnet mask are used. The
settings in the system database are preserved (not erased).
The default IP address is 192.168.1.1 with subnet mask 255.255.255.0 . This is only meant for connection when primary
customer IP is unknown.
l OFF: At boot, the TCP/IP settings configured in the system database are used.
94
Rev AF

Supply Unit Interior
l The laser system will ship with DIP switch 1 set to OFF and the standard IP address pre- configured in the system database.
The other DIP switches are reserved for the internal use. Use Primary configured IP for service in the laser Web Interface.
X19 & X20 - Supply Voltage
The Customer Interface (CI) can receive power and ground either internally or from the customer. Set the jumper configuration
on X19 and X20 to specify which method on supply will be used.
Three modes of supply are available:
l Internal 24V CI Supply Mode: Customer interface supplied by 24V from Chameleon board. Non- isolated.
l Internal 12V CI Supply Mode: Customer Interface supplied by 12V from Chameleon board. Non- isolated.
l External CI Supply Mode: Customer Interface supplied by optional external customer power supply (connect incoming 12-
24V to X28.7 and incoming GND to X28.8). Isolated.
Location of X19 & X20
Power supply control jumpers are located on the Chameleon board next to the connectors for power to Chameleon board (X28).
F IGURE 3- 20: Location of X19 and X20 on Chameleon board.
Jumper Positions
The positions of jumpers on pins X19 and X20 specify what method of supply for the Customer Interface will be used.
95
Rev AF

3140 3340 3640 Technician Training

Do not use any other pin configurations than those detailed in 3340/3140 /3640 documentation.
Preferred configuration is External CI Supply Mode. In this configuration, the CI is completely isolated from the control elec -
tronics on the Chameleon.
When using Internal 12V/24V, the control electronics and the CI are using the same GND potential. Ensure that the external
loads which are connected to the interface (24V_ CI/GND_ CI) do not exceed 250 mA in total.

External Startup
The system can be booted by a remote source by applying a pulse from X28.5 to X28.3. Alternatively, the AutoStart Bridge (X28
pin 3- 5 bridged) will boot the system automatically after switching mains and never powers down after shutdown sequence
(reboots automatically). This bridge can be useful in troubleshooting issues with membrane keypad or cable from membrane
keypad.

Head Interface Card (HIC_ CPA 3x40)
The Head Interface Card (HIC- CPA) is a communications interface between the Chameleon board and the CP- Light board.
The HIC_ CPA is used in 3140/3340/3640 lasers manufactured after 2019, but can also be used in place of HIC- CP Light in older
3140/3340/3640 lasers. The two versions of HIC are interchangeable. The HIC_ CPA is intended to be universal for almost all
Chameleon- based lasers and contains three connectors for cables, though only two of these are used in any one laser.
Connectors
l The HIC plugs directly into the Chameleon board's HIC slot.
l LVDS (Low Voltage Differential Signal) is sent to the marking head from the HIC to CP- Light board through an Ethernet
cable. LVDS is the digital signal used to send galvo control commands which are then interpreted and converted to analog
galvo commands by the CP- Light board.
115
Rev AF

3140 3340 3640 Technician Training
l A shutter connection on the HIC receives data from the galvos and CP- Light board to let the Chameleon know if the mir -
rors are in the Shutter Open or Shutter Closed position.
The HIC to CP- Light signals for the galvos are sent via a CAT7 cable which is a higher performance cable than preceding Ethernet
standards such as CAT5. CAT7 requires each of its twisted wire pairs to be fully shielded. The shielding almost eliminates alien
crosstalk while improving noise resistance.
116
Rev AF

Supply Unit Interior
LEDs
There are two LEDs on the HIC that indicate the shutter status.
F IGURE 3- 26: HIC- CPA LEDs.
Table 3- 19: HIC- CPA LEDs
LED Color State Meaning
Green On Shutter is closed
D1
Yellow On Shutter is open
D4

Light and they can be used interchangeably.

Miscellaneous Interface Controller
(MIC)
The Miscellaneous Interface Controller (MIC) is a daughter board that contains the ports, firmware and hardware for use with
EtherNet/IP or PROFINET. This board is only compatible with the Chameleon MCIP and will not work with any previous versions
of the Chameleon as those version have no connector for the MIC board.
There are two versions of the MIC board: EtherNet/IP and PROFINET. They are not interchangeable and cannot be repro -
grammed in the field. A sticker on one of the RJ45 ports will have an blue dot for EtherNet/IP or yellow dot for PROFINET.
119
Rev AF

3140 3340 3640 Technician Training
The MIC has two RJ45 ports. These should only be used for EtherNet/IP or PROFINET connection and not for TCP/IP connection
such as SmartGraph or TCS+. Those should be connected via the Chameleon board RJ45 ports.
F IGURE 3- 28: MIC- EtherNet/IP with blue dot used to identify the board.
F IGURE 3- 29: MIC- PROFINET board with yellow dot to identify the board.
The MIC board must be enabled and configured in the laser Web Interface if installed.
120
Rev AF

Supply Unit Interior
Certifications and Performance
PROFINET is certified as Conformance Class C and has a cycle rate of 250 microseconds. EtherNet/IP cycle time is not tested.
Performance Metrics such Product Count, Mark Count, Batch Count are not updated in real time, but are updated at least every
100 milliseconds.
Certifications are available on Sharepoint.
Typologies supported:
l Star
l Open Ring (Daisy Chain)
l Closed Ring
Limitations
l CLARiTY Laser Controller cannot be used with EtherNet/IP or PROFINET. SmartGraph, TCS+, and most SARs (including
Ultimate) can be used with EtherNet/IP and PROFINET.
l CPU load is increased by approximately 10% when EtherNet/IP or PROFINET is used.
l Only ASCII characters are supported. No Unicode may be used.
l Maximum of five variable fields per transmission
Files Required for Customer PLC
EtherNet/IP requires the customer to download an EDS file for their PLC setup. There are several versions of the EDS file for down -
load based on the model of laser. Download these from the ODVA marketplace website, from the VTI Support & Technology
team space, or locate the file and user guide on the CD included with the laser or the MIC spare part.
PROFINET requires the customer to download a GSDML file. There is only one version of this file for Videojet lasers. Download
this file from the ProfiBUS website, from the VTI Support & Technology team space, or locate it on the CD included with the laser
or with the MIC spare part.

Umbilical
The supply unit and laser head are connected by an umbilical which carries signals and voltage. Three different umbilical lengths
are available depending on the distance that the laser head will be mounted away from the supply unit. Available umbilical
lengths are:
l 3 meter
l 5 meter
l 10 meter
Connector - Supply Unit Side
The umbilical connects to the supply unit by use of two nuts (one on the inside of supply unit and one on the outside). Signal and
power cables are then connected to the HIC, LIC, terminal block, and Chameleon board.

3140 3340 3640 Technician Training
Harnesses
There are two harnesses inside the laser head.
l Laser Head Harness - Runs from the umbilical connector on the rear of the laser head to the laser tube and CP- Light
board. There are three part numbers for this harness as the 3140 has an additional coaxial connector for the laser tube that
is not present on the 3340. The 3640 has a connector for Fan Speed Control Board that is not present on other models.
l Marking Head Cable - Connects the CP- Light board with the galvos in the marking head. There are many different part
numbers for these harnesses based on the marking head type. Additional part numbers provide various harness wire lengths
as needed for BUSes or BTUs.
Laser Tube
The laser tube, sometimes also called the beam source, is the component that generates the laser beam. The laser tube contains
a sealed cavity filled with CO2 (primarily), small amounts of other gases, and an electronics compartment. Signals and power for
the laser tube are sent via the umbilical from the laser power supply and the Chameleon board via the LIC board. The laser beam
is generated through stimulated emission of radiation wherein RF energy applied to the chamber excites the CO2 atoms.
F IGURE 4- 1: Inside of laser tube sealed gas chamber.
Laser tubes with different combinations of gases create slightly different wavelengths of laser radiation. The 3140/3340/3640
models of laser offer several laser tubes for marking a great number of material types via various wavelengths. The 10, 30, and
60 Watt lasers use different laser tube types and each tube type offers variations with different wavelengths.
132

Laser Head
Order the Correct Tube!
It is important to order the correct laser tube as 10, 30, and 60 Watt tubes are different sizes and have dif -
ferent connectors on them. There are also two generations of 30 Watt laser tube. It is extremely important to
identify which laser tube is used in the 3340 as laser tubes are not backwards compatible!

30 Watt Laser Tubes (V30 and Vi30)
There are two different generations of 30 Watt laser tubes used in 3340 lasers.
l Both 30 Watt laser tubes are manufactured by Synrad.
l Videojet Manufacturing mounts the tube to a fixing plate and aligns them for use in 3340 lasers.
l Both generations of laser tube for 3340 are available in 10.6, 10.2, and 9.3 micron wavelengths.
l The Vi30 laser tube requires CMark version 4.7.5 or newer.
Parts Are Not Interchangeable or Backwards Compatible
It is very important to identify which laser tube is present in the 3340 for both troubleshooting and for spare
parts ordering. The two generations are not interchangeable and they use different power supplies, fans, and
harnesses. Vi30 laser tube requires CMark 4.7.5 or newer to operate without faults.
Identification of 30 Watt Laser Tube Generation (Vi 30 or V30)
There are two different ways to determine which laser tube is in a 3340 laser: 1) Presence of air guides on side of laser tube indic -
ates Vi30 or 2) the type of tube can be viewed in TCS+, the Laser Web Interface, or through the VRS dashboard if the laser has
CMark 4.7.5 or newer.

Vi30 Laser Tube
The Vi30 laser tube is used in 3340 lasers manufactured after spring 2020. It is important to identify which laser tube is used in
the 3340 laser because the parts are not compatible between the Vi30 and the V30. Spare parts are available for both gen -
erations and damaged/broken parts should be replaced with the identical part .
The Vi30 laser tube requires a 48V (624 Watt) power supply and a 48V cooling fan. The power supply must be adjusted based on
umbilical length. The harness which connects the laser tube and fan to the umbilical has a different power connector (3 pole)
than the previous laser tube (2 pole) to prevent connection of wrong tube. CMark 4.7.5 or newer is required when the laser tube
type is Vi30.

laser tube from being damaged by receiving the wrong voltage.

3140 3340 3640 Technician Training
CO2 Laser Wavelength Differences &
Uses
Three laser tubes are available for the 3330/3340/3640 laser and two tubes available for 3120/3320/3130/3140 models. These
tubes vary in the composition of gasses contained within, resulting in different wavelength outputs. Different wavelengths create
different effects on materials.
l 10.6 micron (standard)
l 10.2 micron (not available on 3120/3130/3140 and 3320)
l 9.3 micron
Let's examine the specific uses for the non- standard laser tubes and in what instances each would be selected.
10.6 Micron Laser Tube
The 10.6 micron laser tube is the standard wavelength tube used in the 31x0/33x0 laser. Applications include ablation, micro- frac -
turing.
Uses include ablation (removal of a layer of ink to reveal contrasting color behind) which is used in many food and beverage
applications such as on chipboard boxes (cereal, granola bars, paper labels on bottles). This wavelength is also used in micro- frac -
turing on glass such as on glass bottles or automotive glass. Additionally, the 10.6 micron wavelength can be used on PVC and
may create a color change on this material (sampling required). A code created by ablation is shown in the image below.
The 10.6 micron wavelength is also available on the 3130 model laser.
10.2 Micron Laser Tube (not available on
3120/3320/3130/3140)
The 10.2 micron wavelength is specifically used in applications with laminated cardboard and is often referred to as the "TT" or
"Ten- Two" tube. The standard 10.6 micron tube does not mark well on laminated cardboard and tends to create blistering as air
bubbles form between the cardboard and the lamination. The 10.2 micron wavelength does not cause this blistering and there -
fore results in a more consistent, readable code.
144
Rev AF

Laser Head
F IGURE 4- 14: Comparison of marks made on laminated cardboard with 10.2 μm and 10.6 μm wavelengths.
The 10.2 micron laser tube may also create better codes on certain types of plastics. Sampling is always necessary to determine
the clarity of marking on plastics due to the large variation in composition of plastics between manufacturers.
This tube is not available on 3120/3320/3130/3140 lasers.
9.3 Micron Laser Tube
Often referred to as the "K Tube," the 9.3 μm wavelength is specifically used for marking on clear PET materials. The wavelength
creates a foaming effect on PET as it marks. The bubbles in the foam then collapse on themselves and create a crated effect on
the material. The result is a frosted white code as compared to the standard 10.6 micron wavelength which creates a trench in
the PET and is much less visible on the product. The 9.3 micron wavelength is also capable of creating the higher contrast code
using less power (intensity) and can therefore run at higher line speeds than with 10.6 micron wavelength.
F IGURE 4- 15: Comparison of marks made on PET with 10.6 μm and 9.3 μm wavelengths.
The 9.3 micron wavelength also creates less damage to the PET itself as it does not burn into the material as deeply as the stand -
ard 10.6 micron wavelength. This can be especially important in applications where PET bottles are very thin such as bottled
water where bottles range from 150- 300 micron thickness. The 9.3 micron wavelength generally engraves to a depth of 80
microns where the 9.3 micron wavelength melts to a depth of 20 microns resulting in less material damage.
145
Rev AF

3140 3340 3640 Technician Training
F IGURE 4- 16: Depth comparison of marks made with 10.6 μm and 9.3 μm wavelengths.
The 9.3 micron laser tube is available on all 31x0/33x0/3640 models.
146
Rev AF

Laser Head
Fan Speed Control Board
The 3640 is equipped with a Fan Speed Control Board (FSPC) that plugs into the DB9 connector on the side of the laser tube. The
control board controls the speed of the laser head cooling fan based on the temperature sensed by an attached probe located
along the side of the laser tube. This board is only present on IP54 models as IP65 models will be cooled continuously by external
blower unit rather than fan in laser head.
Do Not Discard
When replacing the laser tube in the 3640 it is important to keep the FSPC board and the attached sensor. The
replacement laser tube will not come with these items.
147
Rev AF

3140 3340 3640 Technician Training
Beam Expander
The beam expander is located in the laser head between the laser tube and the output of the laser head.
The laser beam output from the source generally has a small diameter (d) and without the use of a beam expander results in a
lower irradiance and wider spot size than would be achieved with a beam expander.
A simple trick is to expand the beam through the use of two lenses, one with a negative focal distance and the other with a pos -
itive focal distance. The result of the negative and positive lenses is that there is no focal point between the lenses. This results in
a compact design. This device, called the "Galilean Beam Expander,” allows us to expand the beam to around 5 times the dia -
meter of the beam emerging from the resonator. The difference is reduced as a result of the same proportion, so you can finally
have an almost collimated beam with significant reduction of the spot in the focal point.
.
The beam expander works like a telescope. It contains two lenses which work to expand the diameter of the beam. A wider beam
at the output lens allows for a smaller, denser (and therefore hotter) spot at the point of focus.
148
Rev AF

Laser Head
The following figures show the interior of several beam expanders.
F IGURE 4- 17: Standard Expansion Module for 3340 SHC- 60: beam expansion factor 2.0.
F IGURE 4- 18: Beam expansion Module for 3340 SHC- 120: beam expansion factor 3.6.
149
Rev AF

3140 3340 3640 Technician Training
F IGURE 4- 19: Beam expansion factor: 1 (no lenses).
Standard Configurations
Beam Expanders for 3640
The beam expanders for 3640 laser system have same expansion factors as 3340, but are internally different.
Care should be taken to use correct part number for 3340 versus 3640 laser systems. Use of 3340 beam
expanders in 3640 model may result in power fluctuations and intermittent poor print quality (alternating
darker and lighter codes over time). First lens in 3640 beam expander is tilted slightly and both lenses in 3640
beam expander have a broadband coating. Both of these changes prevent backreflection of the beam into
the laser tube.
150
Rev AF

Laser Head

CP- Light Board
The CP- Light board is located between the laser tube and beam expander. This circuit board contains the drivers that move the
galvos. Signals and voltage from the supply unit are sent to the CP- Light board and then distributed to the X and Y galvos. Pre -
vious models of the Videojet 10 and 30 Watt CO2 3000 series lasers used a Fast Move (FM) Board. In 3340/3140 and 3640 mod -
els, the CP- Light board is analogous to that FM Board. The CP- Light board is not backwards compatible with ACC3- based
systems.
152
Rev AF

Laser Head
Overview
F IGURE 4- 20: CP- Light board - Top View.
153
Rev AF

3140 3340 3640 Technician Training
CP- Light versus CP- Light 2 and CP- Light 4
There are three generations of CP- Light board. These different generations must be matched with the correct
galvos and marking head cable or the set will not function. All CP- Light versions serve the same purpose, but
wiring from the galvos is different between the generations. Programming is also distinct between the three.
CP- Light 4 has a bigger EEPROM which allows for additional Clearance Move programming. The Clearance
Move helps to lubricate bearings across the entire galvo movement range upon each Stop.
For more information on identifying CP- Light generation and the corresponding galvos and marking head
cable, see "Identifying Marking Head Types" on page 165 .
l The CP- Light board receives signals from the Chameleon via the HIC (Head Interface Controller). This includes Low Voltage
Differential Signal (LVDS) and Shutter control.
l The 48V power supply in the supply unit provides power to the CP- Light board.
l LVDS signals are sent from the HIC to the CP- Light board via a CAT7 cable .
l The LVDS signal commands for moving the galvos which are generated by the Chameleon and transmitted through the
HIC.
l The X and Y- mirrors are driven using Pulse Width Modulation using drivers on the back of board and sent via X- and Y- mirror
current connectors on front of board.
l CP- Light 2/4 also receive temperature information from a thermistor attached to the galvo block in the marking head.
l The power for the pilot laser, if one is installed, is supplied by the CP- Light.
Pilot Laser is Class 2
The pilot laser does not mark on the substrate. The pilot laser is a Class 2 laser. See "Laser Clas -
sification" on page 37 for details on Laser Classes.
Hardware switches located in the marking head detect whether the shutter is open or closed and send signals back to the CP-
Light. The X- and Y- mirror each have an individual cable for feedback on the shutter position from the specified galvo. The safety
circuitry monitors feedback from these hardware switches for the shutter signal. If there are faults with the shutter the system
will not allow the laser tube to energize nor the emission LED to illuminate.
154
Rev AF

Laser Head
CP- Light Board LEDs
There are several diagnostic LEDs on the CP- Light board that indicate presence of voltage and signals. Pulse Width Modulation
(PWM) drives the galvos to marking and shutter positions. The top row of LEDs (D203- 207) illuminate to indicate PWM voltages
are present and should always be illuminated after the laser unit has booted.

Spare Parts Replacement
Important to know about replacing CP- Light (2/4) and galvo block:
156
Rev AF

Laser Head
Do Not Break Up Sets
The CP- Light and galvo block come as a set and should not be separated. Errors and/or poor marking quality
will occur if matching set is not installed in laser.
Model Specific After Install/Boot
Though the 3140 and 3340 share the same part numbers, once the CP- Light has been installed in a laser and
booted a code is written to the CP- Light that will thereafter only allow it to connect with a supply unit of the
matching model. For example, if a galvo set/CP- Light is installed in a 3340 and booted it can only be used in a
3340 from that point on. Wrong LIC Detected E00212 error will occur if that CP- Light/Galvo set is used in a
3140 or 3640 after this code has been written. This code cannot be altered except in TCS+ with CMark 4.9 or
newer (Reset CP- Light in Laser Unit Settings). It is used as a safety to avoid connecting a 3640 with 50V laser
power supply or a 3340 with Vi30 with 48V laser power supply to a 3140/3340 where laser tube is expecting
30V.
The galvo block for older 3640 has additional metal parts that are used to guard the marking head cable from high energy reflec -
tion within the marking head. These metal parts are attached to the spare galvo block when it arrives. No additional action is
necessary.
All 3x40 models (Marking Head Type D or newer) have a solid galvo block that protects the cables in the marking head.
157
Rev AF

Marking Head
The marking head contains the components necessary to direct the laser beam in order to create the markings. The marking
head components consist of an X- and Y- mirror/galvanometer assembly, a red emissions LED, and optionally a pilot laser. These
components are attached by a marking head cable that varies with the specific head type. The marking head type is selected as
part of the original laser system order.
There are four marking head types available.
l SHC 60c/d/e (6 mm mirrors)
l SHC 100c/d/e (10 mm mirrors)
l SHC 120c (12 mm mirrors)
l SHC 150c (15 mm mirrors) - not available on 3140
The marking head type is chosen to fit the application need. The marking head type choice is based on many factors including
type of substrate, desired marking field size, desired spot size, and many other factors.
Each head type has an associated set of lenses that are available for the specific head type. See "Mark Fields" on page 176 for
more information on available lenses for each head type. Lenses for the SHC 60 are contained within a bayonet style holder that
is removed by turning the holder. The SHC 100/120/150 lens holder requires use of an Allen wrench to remove two screws hold -
ing the lens holder to the marking head.
The SHC60 and SHC100 marking heads have three generations (c, d, and e) which are differentiated by the components used.
When replacing components on the SHC60 and SHC100 marking heads it is extremely important to identify which generation
components are used and the proper spare parts to be used for replacement as well as minimum software versions necessary for
each generation.
Marking Head Exterior
The marking head exterior contains 4 screws that hold the cover in place. There is also a red LED on the front of the marking
head that gives a visual indication of when laser power is enabled. If this emission LED or the one on the membrane keypad do
not function, then the laser will not be enabled. The emissions LEDs are part of the safety logic for the system.
159

each of the major components are provided individually.
